title="Scroll">scroll</a>, leaning on a <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">saltire</a>, fishing net</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Barbados" title="Barbados">Barbados</a>, <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">Georgia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greece" title="Greece">Greece</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cyprus" title="Cyprus">Cyprus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew_in_Romania" title="Saint Andrew in Romania">Romania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Patras" title="Patras">Patras</a>, <a href="/wiki/Burgundy" title="Burgundy">Burgundy</a>, <a href="/wiki/San_Andr%C3%A9s,_Santa_Cruz_de_Tenerife" title="San Andrés, Santa Cruz de Tenerife">San Andrés (Tenerife)</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Para%C3%B1aque" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Parañaque">Diocese of Parañaque</a>, <a href="/wiki/Candaba" title="Candaba">Candaba</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pampanga" title="Pampanga">Pampanga</a>, <a href="/wiki/Masinloc" title="Masinloc">Masinloc</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Telhado&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Telhado (page does not exist)">Telhado</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://pt.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telhado_(Fund%C3%A3o)" class="extiw" title="pt:Telhado (Fundão)">pt</a>&#93;</span>, <a href="/wiki/Sarzana" title="Sarzana">Sarzana</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-cattedraledisarzana.it_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cattedraledisarzana.it-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pienza" title="Pienza">Pienza</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliamsMaxwell2018300_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsMaxwell2018300-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
 <a href="/wiki/Amalfi" title="Amalfi">Amalfi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Luqa" title="Luqa">Luqa</a> (<a href="/wiki/Malta" title="Malta">Malta</a>), <a href="/wiki/Manila" title="Manila">Manila</a><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussia</a>; <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Victoria_in_Canada" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Victoria in Canada">Diocese of Victoria</a>; fishermen, fishmongers and rope-makers, textile workers, singers, miners, pregnant women, butchers, farm workers, protection against sore throats, protection against convulsions, protection against fever, protection against whooping cough, Russian Navy, <a href="/wiki/Table_of_authorities" title="Table of authorities">Tables of Authority</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Andrew the Apostle</b> (<a href="/wiki/Koin%C4%93_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Koinē Greek language">Koinē Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Ἀνδρέᾱς</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Koinē Greek-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn">Andréās</i></span> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="el-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Greek" title="Help:IPA/Greek">[anˈdre.aːs̠]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Andrēās</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="la-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin" title="Help:IPA/Latin">[än̪ˈd̪reː.äːs]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Aramaic" title="Aramaic">Aramaic</a>: <span lang="jpa" dir="rtl">אַנדּרֵאוָס</span>, <a href="/wiki/Classical_Syriac_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Classical Syriac language">Classical Syriac</a>: <span lang="syc" dir="rtl">ܐܰܢܕ݁ܪܶܐܘܳܣ</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Classical Syriac-language romanization"><i lang="syc-Latn">ʾAnd’reʾwās</i></span><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>), also called <b>Saint Andrew</b>, was an <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">apostle of Jesus</a>. He is the brother of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Simon Peter</a><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and is a son of Jonah. He is referred to in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> tradition as <b>the First-Called</b> (<span title="Koinē Greek-language text"><span lang="grc">Πρωτόκλητος</span></span>, <span title="Koinē Greek-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn">Prōtoklētos</i></span>).
</p><p>According to Eastern Orthodox tradition, the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_successor" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolic successor">apostolic successor</a> to Andrew is the <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriarch of Constantinople">Patriarch of Constantinople</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The name "<a href="/wiki/Andrew_(name)" class="mw-redirect" title="Andrew (name)">Andrew</a>" (meaning <i>manly, brave</i>, from <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">ἀνδρεία</span></span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">translit.</a></small>&#160;<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn"><i>andreía</i></i></span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Literal_translation" title="Literal translation">lit.</a></small>&#160;"manhood, valour"), like other Greek names, appears to have been common among the <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jews</a> and other <a href="/wiki/Hellenization" title="Hellenization">Hellenized</a> people since the second or third century B.C.<sup id="cite_ref-MacRory_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacRory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> No <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a> or <a href="/wiki/Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Aramaic language">Aramaic</a> name is recorded for him.
</p><p>Andrew the Apostle was born between 5 and 10 AD<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Bethsaida" title="Bethsaida">Bethsaida</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Galilee" title="Galilee">Galilee</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Indep_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Indep-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> states that Andrew was the brother of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Simon Peter</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-butler_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-butler-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> and likewise a son of Jonah. "The first striking characteristic of Andrew is his name: it is not Hebrew, as might have been expected, but Greek, indicative of a certain cultural openness in his family that cannot be ignored. We are in Galilee, where the Greek language and culture are quite present."<sup id="cite_ref-Benedict_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benedict-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_(Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610)_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_%28Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610%29_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg/220px-Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_%28Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610%29_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_%28Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610%29_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg/330px-Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_%28Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610%29_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_%28Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610%29_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg/440px-Michelangelo_Merisi_da_Caravaggio_%28Milan_1571-Port%27_Ercole_1610%29_-_The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew_-_RCIN_402824_-_Hampton_Court_Palace.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2074" data-file-height="1650" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/The_Calling_of_Saints_Peter_and_Andrew" title="The Calling of Saints Peter and Andrew">The Calling of Saints Peter and Andrew</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Caravaggio" title="Caravaggio">Caravaggio</a> (1603–1606)</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="With_Jesus">With Jesus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: With Jesus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Both he and his brother Peter were fishermen by trade, hence the tradition that <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> called them to be his <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciples</a> by saying that he will make them "<a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">fishers of men</a>" (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">ἁλιεῖς ἀνθρώπων</span></span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">translit.</a></small>&#160;<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn"><i>halieîs anthrṓpōn</i></i></span>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMetzgerCoogan199327_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetzgerCoogan199327-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> According to Mark 1:29, at the beginning of Jesus' public life, they occupied the same house at <a href="/wiki/Capernaum" title="Capernaum">Capernaum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-MacRory_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacRory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Gospel of Mark</a><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Simon Peter and Andrew were both called together to become disciples of Jesus and "fishers of men". These narratives record that Jesus was walking along the shore of the Sea of Galilee, observed Simon and Andrew fishing, and called them to discipleship.
</p><p>In the parallel incident in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Andrew is not named, nor is reference made to Simon having a brother. In this narrative, Jesus initially used a boat, solely described as being Simon's, as a platform for preaching to the multitudes on the shore and then as a means to achieving a huge trawl of fish on a night which had hitherto proved fruitless. The narrative indicates that Simon was not the only fisherman in the boat (<i>they signalled to their partners in the other boat …</i><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> but it is not until the next chapter<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> that Andrew is named as Simon's brother. However, it is generally understood that Andrew was fishing with Simon on the night in question. <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Poole" title="Matthew Poole">Matthew Poole</a>, in his <i>Annotations on the Holy Bible</i>, stressed that 'Luke denies not that Andrew was there'.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a> states that Andrew was a disciple of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John the Baptist</a>, whose testimony first led him, and another unnamed disciple of John the Baptist, to follow Jesus. Andrew at once recognized Jesus as the <a href="/wiki/Messiah" title="Messiah">Messiah</a>, and hastened to introduce him to his brother.<sup id="cite_ref-shrine_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-shrine-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Byzantine Church honours him with the name <i>Protokletos</i>, which means "the first called".<sup id="cite_ref-Benedict_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benedict-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Thenceforth, the two brothers were disciples of Christ. On a subsequent occasion, prior to the final call to the <a href="/wiki/Apostolates" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostolates">apostolate</a>, they were called to a closer companionship, and then they left all things to follow Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-MacRory_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacRory-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Subsequently, in the gospels, Andrew is referred to as being present on some important occasions as one of the disciples more closely attached to Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> Andrew told Jesus about the boy with the loaves and fishes,<sup id="cite_ref-Benedict_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Benedict-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and when certain Greeks went to see Jesus, they came to Philip, but Philip then had recourse to Andrew.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Andrew was present at the <a href="/wiki/Last_Supper" title="Last Supper">Last Supper</a>. Andrew was one of the four disciples who came to Jesus on the <a href="/wiki/Mount_of_Olives" title="Mount of Olives">Mount of Olives</a> to ask about the signs of <a href="/wiki/Parousia" class="mw-redirect" title="Parousia">Jesus' return</a> at the "end of the age".<sup id="cite_ref-vanews_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vanews-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="After_Jesus">After Jesus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: After Jesus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> in his <i><a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Church History</a></i> 3.1 (4th century) quoted <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a> (c. 185 – c. 253) as saying that Andrew preached in <a href="/wiki/Scythia" title="Scythia">Scythia</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Primary_Chronicle" title="Primary Chronicle">Chronicle of Nestor</a> (1113) adds that he preached along the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Sea</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Dnieper_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Dnieper River">Dnieper</a> river as far as <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a>, and from there he travelled to <a href="/wiki/Veliky_Novgorod" title="Veliky Novgorod">Novgorod</a>. Hence, he became a <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Romania" title="Romania">Romania</a> and <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>. According to <a href="/wiki/Hippolytus_of_Rome" title="Hippolytus of Rome">Hippolytus of Rome</a>, Andrew preached in <a href="/wiki/Thrace" title="Thrace">Thrace</a>, and his presence in Byzantium is mentioned in the apocryphal <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Andrew" title="Acts of Andrew">Acts of Andrew</a>. According to tradition, he founded the <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople#History" title="Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople">see of Byzantium</a> (later <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>) in AD 38, installing <a href="/wiki/Stachys_the_Apostle" title="Stachys the Apostle">Stachys</a> as bishop. 
This diocese became the seat of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_ecumenical_patriarchs_of_Constantinople" title="List of ecumenical patriarchs of Constantinople">Patriarchate of Constantinople</a> under <a href="/wiki/Anatolius_of_Constantinople" title="Anatolius of Constantinople">Anatolius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon#The_status_of_Constantinople" title="Council of Chalcedon">in 451</a>.
Andrew, along with Stachys, is recognized as the patron saint of the Patriarchate.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Seleucia" title="Basil of Seleucia">Basil of Seleucia</a> (5th century) also knew of Apostle Andrew's missions in Thrace, Scythia and <a href="/wiki/Achaea_(Roman_province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Achaea (Roman province)">Achaea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson201351_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson201351-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Martyrdom">Martyrdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Martyrdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Andrew is said to have been <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyrs" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian martyrs">martyred</a> by <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion" title="Crucifixion">crucifixion</a> at the city of <a href="/wiki/Patras" title="Patras">Patras</a> (Patræ) in <a href="/wiki/Achaea" title="Achaea">Achaea</a>, in AD 60.<sup id="cite_ref-vanews_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vanews-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Early texts, such as the Acts of Andrew known to <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Tours" title="Gregory of Tours">Gregory of Tours</a><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> (6th century), describe Andrew as bound, not nailed, to a <a href="/wiki/Christian_cross" title="Christian cross">Latin cross</a> of the kind on which Jesus is said to have been crucified; yet a tradition developed that Andrew had been crucified on a cross of the form called <i><a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">crux decussata</a></i> (X-shaped cross, or "saltire"), now commonly known as a "<a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">Saint Andrew's Cross</a>" — supposedly at his own request, as he deemed himself unworthy to be crucified on the same type of cross as Jesus had been.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Iconography" title="Iconography">iconography</a> of the martyrdom of Andrew — showing him bound to an X-shaped cross — does not appear to have been standardized until the later <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECalvert1984545note_12_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECalvert1984545note_12-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_(Menologion_of_Basil_II).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Andrew_%28Menologion_of_Basil_II%29.jpg/220px-Andrew_%28Menologion_of_Basil_II%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Andrew_%28Menologion_of_Basil_II%29.jpg/330px-Andrew_%28Menologion_of_Basil_II%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Andrew_%28Menologion_of_Basil_II%29.jpg/440px-Andrew_%28Menologion_of_Basil_II%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1078" data-file-height="744" /></a><figcaption>The crucifixion of Andrew the Apostle. Miniature from the <a href="/wiki/Menologion_of_Basil_II" title="Menologion of Basil II">Menologion of Basil II</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Acts_of_Andrew">The Acts of Andrew</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: The Acts of Andrew">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Andrew" title="Acts of Andrew">Acts of Andrew</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Apocrypha" title="Apocrypha">apocryphal</a> <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Andrew" title="Acts of Andrew">Acts of Andrew</a>, mentioned by <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">Epiphanius</a> and others, is among a disparate group of Acts of the Apostles that were traditionally attributed to <a href="/wiki/Leucius_Charinus" title="Leucius Charinus">Leucius Charinus</a>, but it shows several signs of a mid-2nd-century origin.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> It describes the supposed travels of the title character, the miracles he performed during them, and finally a description of his martyrdom. <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius of Caesarea</a> knew the work, which he dismissed as the product of a <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heretic</a> and absurd.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> The Acts, as well as a <i>Gospel of St Andrew</i>, appear among rejected books in the <i><a href="/wiki/Decretum_Gelasianum" class="mw-redirect" title="Decretum Gelasianum">Decretum Gelasianum</a></i> connected with the name of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gelasius_I" title="Pope Gelasius I">Pope Gelasius I</a>. <a href="/wiki/Dennis_MacDonald" title="Dennis MacDonald">Dennis MacDonald</a> posits the theory that the non-canonical Acts of Andrew was a Christian retelling of Homer's Odyssey.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg/220px-Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg/330px-Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg/440px-Patras_Cathedral_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/St_Andrew%27s_Cathedral,_Patras" class="mw-redirect" title="St Andrew&#39;s Cathedral, Patras">Saint Andrew of Patras cathedral</a>, where Saint Andrew's relics are kept</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/170px-Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/255px-Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/340px-Andreas_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1818" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Andrew in the <a href="/wiki/Archbasilica_of_St._John_Lateran" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbasilica of St. John Lateran">Archbasilica of St. John Lateran</a> by <a href="/wiki/Camillo_Rusconi" title="Camillo Rusconi">Camillo Rusconi</a> (1713–1715)</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">Relics</a> alleged to be those of the Apostle Andrew are kept at the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew_of_Patras" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Andrew of Patras">Basilica of Saint Andrew</a> in <a href="/wiki/Patras" title="Patras">Patras</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greece" title="Greece">Greece</a>; in <a href="/wiki/Amalfi_Cathedral" title="Amalfi Cathedral">Amalfi Cathedral</a> (the Duomo di Sant'Andrea), <a href="/wiki/Amalfi" title="Amalfi">Amalfi</a> and in <a href="/wiki/Sarzana_Cathedral" title="Sarzana Cathedral">Sarzana Cathedral</a><sup id="cite_ref-cattedraledisarzana.it_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cattedraledisarzana.it-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Sarzana" title="Sarzana">Sarzana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>; <a href="/wiki/St_Mary%27s_Cathedral,_Edinburgh_(Catholic)" title="St Mary&#39;s Cathedral, Edinburgh (Catholic)">St Mary's Cathedral</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-shrine_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-shrine-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> and the Church of St Andrew and St Albert, <a href="/wiki/Warsaw" title="Warsaw">Warsaw</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a>. There are also numerous smaller <a href="/wiki/Reliquaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Reliquaries">reliquaries</a> throughout the world.
</p><p>Andrew's remains were preserved at Patras. According to one legend, Regulus (Rule), a monk at Patras, was advised in a dream to hide some of the bones. Shortly thereafter, most of the relics were transferred from <a href="/wiki/Patras" title="Patras">Patras</a> to <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> by order of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_emperor" title="Roman emperor">Roman emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Constantius_II" title="Constantius II">Constantius II</a> around 357 and deposited in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Apostles" title="Church of the Holy Apostles">Church of the Holy Apostles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacRory1907_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacRory1907-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Regulus was said to have had a second dream in which an angel advised him to take the hidden relics "to the ends of the earth" for protection. Wherever he was shipwrecked, he was to build a shrine for them. St. Rule set sail, taking with him a kneecap, an upper arm bone, three fingers and a tooth. He sailed west, towards the edge of the known world, and was shipwrecked on the coast of <a href="/wiki/Fife,_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="Fife, Scotland">Fife, Scotland</a>. However, the relics were probably brought to Britain in 597 as part of the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_mission" title="Gregorian mission">Augustine Mission</a>, and then in 732 to Fife, by Bishop <a href="/wiki/Acca_of_Hexham" title="Acca of Hexham">Acca of Hexham</a>, a well-known collector of religious relics.<sup id="cite_ref-shrine_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-shrine-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The skull of Saint Andrew, which had been taken to Constantinople, was returned to Patras by Emperor <a href="/wiki/Basil_I" title="Basil I">Basil I</a>, who ruled from 867 to 886.<sup id="cite_ref-Christodoulou_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Christodoulou-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1208, following the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Constantinople" title="Sack of Constantinople">sack of Constantinople</a>, those relics of Saint Andrew and Saint Peter which remained in the imperial city were taken to <a href="/wiki/Amalfi" title="Amalfi">Amalfi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Capua_the_Elder" title="Peter of Capua the Elder">Peter of Capua</a>, a native of Amalfi. A <a href="/wiki/Amalfi_Cathedral" title="Amalfi Cathedral">cathedral</a> was built, dedicated to Saint Andrew, as is the town itself, to house a tomb in its crypt where it is maintained that most of the relics of the apostle, including an occipital bone, remain.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Palaeologus" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Palaeologus">Thomas Palaeologus</a> was the youngest surviving son of Byzantine Emperor Manuel II Palaiologos. Thomas ruled the province of <a href="/wiki/Morea" title="Morea">Morea</a>, the medieval name for the Peloponnese. In 1461, when the Ottomans crossed the Strait of Corinth, Palaeologus fled Patras for exile in Italy, bringing with him what was purported to be the skull of Saint Andrew. He gave the head to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_II" title="Pope Pius II">Pope Pius II</a>, who had it enshrined in one of the four central piers of <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Palace" title="Apostolic Palace">Vatican</a> and then in <a href="/wiki/Pienza" title="Pienza">Pienza</a>, Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliamsMaxwell2018300_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsMaxwell2018300-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1964, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a>, as a gesture of goodwill toward the Greek Orthodox Church, ordered that the one relic of Saint Andrew held in <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City" title="Vatican City">Vatican City</a> be returned to Patras. Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Augustin_Bea" title="Augustin Bea">Augustin Bea</a>, head of the Vatican's <a href="/wiki/Secretariat_for_Promoting_Christian_Unity" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretariat for Promoting Christian Unity">Secretariat for Promoting Christian Unity</a>, led a delegation that presented the skull to Bishop Constantine of Patras on 24 September 1964.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The cross of Saint Andrew was taken from Greece during the Crusades by the Duke of Burgundy.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDenoël2004_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDenoël2004-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> It was kept in the <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_St._Victor,_Marseille" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of St. Victor, Marseille">church of St. Victor</a> in Marseilles<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> until it returned to Patras on 19 January 1980. The cross of the apostle was presented to the Bishop of Patras Nicodemus by a Catholic delegation led by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Roger_Etchegaray" title="Roger Etchegaray">Roger Etchegaray</a>. All the relics, which consist of the small finger, the skull (part of the top of the cranium of Saint Andrew), and the cross on which he was <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyred</a>, have been kept in the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew_of_Patras" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Andrew of Patras">Church of St. Andrew</a> at Patras in a special shrine and are revered in a special ceremony every 30 November, his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a>.
</p><p>In 2006, the Catholic Church, again through Cardinal Etchegaray, gave the Greek Orthodox Church another relic of Saint Andrew.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Liturgical_Commemoration">Liturgical Commemoration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Liturgical Commemoration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Eastern Orthodox Church commemorates the Apostle Andrew on several days throughout the Church Calendar. Fixed days of commemoration include:
</p>
<ul><li>June 20 - The Translation of the Relics of the Apostles Andrew, Thomas, and Luke; the Prophet Elisha; and the Martyr Lazarus.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>June 30 - The Twelve Apostles.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>September 26 - The Translation of the skull of Andrew in 1964.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>November 30 - Primary Feast Day.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>There are also days which are movable:
</p>
<ul><li>The Sunday before November 30 - Synaxis of the Saints of Achaea.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The Sunday of the Samaritan Woman - Synaxis of the all the Holy Fathers, Archbishops, and Patriarchs of Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Traditions_and_legends">Traditions and legends</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Traditions and legends">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Georgia">Georgia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Georgia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Andrew_fresco_(Kintsvisi,_Georgia).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/St_Andrew_fresco_%28Kintsvisi%2C_Georgia%29.JPG/220px-St_Andrew_fresco_%28Kintsvisi%2C_Georgia%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a0/St_Andrew_fresco_%28Kintsvisi%2C_Georgia%29.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="318" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption>A 13th-century fresco depicting Saint Andrew, from <a href="/wiki/Kintsvisi_Monastery" title="Kintsvisi Monastery">Kintsvisi Monastery</a>, Georgia</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Georgian_Orthodox_Church" title="Georgian Orthodox Church">church tradition of Georgia</a> regards Andrew as the first preacher of Christianity in the territory of Georgia and as the founder of the Georgian church. This tradition derives from Byzantine sources, particularly <a href="/wiki/Nicetas_of_Paphlagonia" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicetas of Paphlagonia">Nicetas of Paphlagonia</a> (died c. 890) who asserts that "Andrew preached to the <a href="/wiki/Caucasian_Iberia" class="mw-redirect" title="Caucasian Iberia">Iberians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sarmatians" title="Sarmatians">Sauromatians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Taurica" class="mw-redirect" title="Taurica">Taurians</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Scythians" title="Scythians">Scythians</a> and to every region and city, on the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Sea</a>, both north and south."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPeterson195820_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeterson195820-49">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The version was adopted by the 10th–11th-century Georgian ecclesiastics and, refurbished with more details, was inserted in the <a href="/wiki/Georgian_Chronicles" class="mw-redirect" title="Georgian Chronicles">Georgian Chronicles</a>. The story of Andrew's mission in the Georgian lands endowed the Georgian church with apostolic origin and served as a defence argument to <a href="/wiki/George_the_Hagiorite" title="George the Hagiorite">George the Hagiorite</a> against the encroachments from the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Antioch" title="Church of Antioch">Antiochian church</a> authorities on <a href="/wiki/Autocephaly" title="Autocephaly">autocephaly</a> of the Georgian church. Another Georgian monk, <a href="/wiki/Ephrem_Mtsire" title="Ephrem Mtsire">Ephraim the Minor</a>, produced a thesis, reconciling Andrew's story with an earlier evidence of the 4th-century conversion of Georgians by <a href="/wiki/Saint_Nino" title="Saint Nino">Nino</a> and explaining the necessity of the "second Christening" by Nino. The thesis was made canonical by the Georgian church council in 1103.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERapp2003433_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERapp2003433-50">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDjobadze197682–83_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDjobadze197682–83-51">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The Georgian Orthodox Church marks two feast days in honour of Saint Andrew, on 12 May and 13 December. The former date, dedicated to Andrew's arrival in Georgia, is a <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Georgia_(country)" class="mw-redirect" title="Public holidays in Georgia (country)">public holiday in Georgia</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cyprus">Cyprus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Cyprus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Cypriot tradition holds that a ship which was transporting Andrew went off course and ran aground. Upon coming ashore, Andrew struck the rocks with his staff at which point a spring of healing waters gushed forth. Using it, the sight of the ship's captain, who had been blind in one eye, was restored. Thereafter, the site became a place of pilgrimage and a fortified monastery, the <a href="/wiki/Apostolos_Andreas_Monastery" title="Apostolos Andreas Monastery">Apostolos Andreas Monastery</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Apostolos_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apostolos-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> stood there in the 12th century, from which <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Comnenus_of_Cyprus" class="mw-redirect" title="Isaac Comnenus of Cyprus">Isaac Comnenus</a> negotiated his surrender to <a href="/wiki/Richard_I_of_England" title="Richard I of England">Richard the Lionheart</a>. In the 15th century, a small chapel was built close to the shore. The main monastery of the current church dates to the 18th century.
</p><p>Other pilgrimages are more recent. The story is told that in 1895, the son of a Maria Georgiou was kidnapped. Seventeen years later, Andrew appeared to her in a dream, telling her to pray for her son's return at the monastery. Living in Anatolia, she embarked on the crossing to Cyprus on a very crowded boat. As she was telling her story during the journey, one of the passengers, a young Dervish priest, became more and more interested. Asking if her son had any distinguishing marks, he stripped off his clothes to reveal the same marks and mother and son were thus reunited.<sup id="cite_ref-Apostolos_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Apostolos-52">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Apostolos_Andreas_Monastery" title="Apostolos Andreas Monastery">Apostolos Andreas Monastery</a> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Απόστολος Ανδρέας</span>) is a <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monastery</a> dedicated to Saint Andrew situated just south of <a href="/wiki/Cape_Apostolos_Andreas" title="Cape Apostolos Andreas">Cape Apostolos Andreas</a>, which is the north-easternmost point of the island of <a href="/wiki/Cyprus" title="Cyprus">Cyprus</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Rizokarpaso" title="Rizokarpaso">Rizokarpaso</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Karpass_Peninsula" class="mw-redirect" title="Karpass Peninsula">Karpass Peninsula</a>. The monastery is an important site to the <a href="/wiki/Cypriot_Orthodox_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Cypriot Orthodox Church">Cypriot Orthodox Church</a>. It was once known as 'the Lourdes of Cyprus', served not by an organized community of monks but by a changing group of volunteer priests and laymen. Both <a href="/wiki/Greek_Cypriot" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Cypriot">Greek Cypriot</a> and <a href="/wiki/Turkish_Cypriot" class="mw-redirect" title="Turkish Cypriot">Turkish Cypriot</a> communities consider the monastery a holy place. As such, it is visited by many people for votive prayers.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Malta">Malta</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Malta">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Niche_of_St._Andrew_(Luqa).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Niche_of_St._Andrew_%28Luqa%29.jpg/220px-Niche_of_St._Andrew_%28Luqa%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Niche_of_St._Andrew_%28Luqa%29.jpg/330px-Niche_of_St._Andrew_%28Luqa%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Niche_of_St._Andrew_%28Luqa%29.jpg/440px-Niche_of_St._Andrew_%28Luqa%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="908" data-file-height="679" /></a><figcaption>Niche of St. Andrew (Luqa)</figcaption></figure>
<p>St. Andrew (Sant' Andrija) is the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Luqa" title="Luqa">Luqa</a>. The patron saint's traditional feast is celebrated on the first Sunday of July, with the liturgical feast being celebrated on 30 November.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> A local niche dedicated to him is found in Luqa, which is two storeys high. The first reference regards the small chapel at <a href="/wiki/Luqa" title="Luqa">Luqa</a> dedicated to Andrew dates to 1497. This chapel contained three altars, one of them dedicated to Andrew. The painting showing <i>Mary with Saints Andrew and Paul</i> was painted by the Maltese artist Filippo Dingli. At one time, many fishermen lived in the village of Luqa, and this may be the main reason for choosing Andrew as patron saint. The statue of Andrew was sculpted in wood by Giuseppe Scolaro in 1779. This statue underwent several restoration works including that of 1913 performed by the Maltese artist Abraham Gatt. The <i>Martyrdom of Saint Andrew</i> on the main altar of the church was painted by <a href="/wiki/Mattia_Preti" title="Mattia Preti">Mattia Preti</a> in 1687.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Romania">Romania</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Romania">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG/220px-Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG/330px-Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG/440px-Pe%C8%99terile_Sf._Apostol_Andrei_-_detalii_01.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>Entrance to Saint Andrew's caves near Ion Corvin, <a href="/wiki/Constan%C8%9Ba_County" title="Constanța County">Constanța County</a></figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew_in_Romania" title="Saint Andrew in Romania">Saint Andrew in Romania</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_Romania" title="History of Christianity in Romania">History of Christianity in Romania</a></div>
<p>Saint Andrew is the patron saint of Romania. The official stance of the <a href="/wiki/Romanian_Orthodox_Church" title="Romanian Orthodox Church">Romanian Orthodox Church</a> is that Andrew preached the Gospel in the province of <a href="/wiki/Dobruja" title="Dobruja">Dobruja</a> (<a href="/wiki/Scythia_Minor_(Roman_province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Scythia Minor (Roman province)">Scythia Minor</a>) to the <a href="/wiki/Daco-Roman" title="Daco-Roman">Daco-Romans</a> (romanized subgroup of the <a href="/wiki/Thracians" title="Thracians">Thracians</a>, ancestors to the Romanians as per the Daco-Roman <a href="/wiki/Ethnogenesis" title="Ethnogenesis">origin theory</a>), whom he is said to have converted to Christianity. Such a tradition was however not widely acknowledged until the 20th century,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> although substantiated by the <a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)"><i>Church History</i> of Eusebius</a>.
</p><p>According to Hippolyte of Antioch, (died c. 250 C.E.) in his On Apostles, Origen in the third book of his <i>Commentaries on the Genesis</i> (254 C.E.), Eusebius of Caesarea in his Church History (340 C.E.), and other sources, such as Usaard's Martyrdom written between 845 and 865, and Jacobus de Voragine's <i>Golden Legend</i> (c. 1260), Andrew preached in Scythia, a possible reference to <a href="/wiki/Scythia_Minor_(Roman_province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Scythia Minor (Roman province)">Scythia Minor</a>, corresponding to the modern-day regions of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Dobruja" title="Northern Dobruja">Northern Dobruja</a> (part of Romania) and <a href="/wiki/Southern_Dobruja" title="Southern Dobruja">Southern Dobruja</a> (part of Bulgaria). According to Hippolyte of Antioch, Saint Andrew had also preached to the Thracians.
</p><p>Although the exclusive presence in the Romanian language of Latin vocabulary for concepts of Christian faith may indicate the antiquity of Daco-Roman Christianity,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> according to some modern Romanian scholars, the idea of early Christianisation (preceding the <a href="/wiki/Edict_of_Milan" title="Edict of Milan">Edict of Milan</a>) is unsustainable. They take the idea to be a part of the ideology of <a href="/wiki/Protochronism" class="mw-redirect" title="Protochronism">protochronism</a>, which they claim to purport that the Eastern Orthodox Church has been a companion and defender of the Romanian people for its entire history, aspect supposedly used for propaganda purposes during the communist era.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStanTurcescu200748_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStanTurcescu200748-56">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Historians such as <a href="/wiki/Ioan-Aurel_Pop" title="Ioan-Aurel Pop">Ioan-Aurel Pop</a> consider Romanians to be the first to adopt Christianity among the peoples which now inhabit the territories bordering Romania,<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> conversion to Christianity until the third century (in the province of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Dacia" title="Roman Dacia">Dacia Traiana</a>, dissolved c. 271/275 AD) playing a significant part in the <a href="/wiki/Origin_of_the_Romanians" title="Origin of the Romanians">ethnogenesis of the Romanians</a>.
</p><p>Scholar <a href="/wiki/Mircea_Eliade" title="Mircea Eliade">Mircea Eliade</a> argues in favor of structural links between <a href="/wiki/Dacian_religion" class="mw-redirect" title="Dacian religion">Zamolxism</a> and Christianity,<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> thus suggesting a higher likelihood of early conversion. As such, if Andrew the Apostle had preached in Dobruja (in proximity to the Thracians he had also preached to) and not in Crimea as per the Russian Orthodox Church, Christianity in Romania can be considered of apostolic origin.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-54">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between the 4th and 6th centuries, the region of Scythia Minor played an influential role in the development of <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christian</a> theology.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ukraine">Ukraine</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Ukraine">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/5/59/Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg/220px-Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/5/59/Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg/330px-Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/5/59/Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg/440px-Predskazanie_Kiev_sv_Andrey.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="519" /></a><figcaption>Saint Andrew's prophecy of Kiev, depicted in the <i><a href="/wiki/Radzivill_Chronicle" class="mw-redirect" title="Radzivill Chronicle">Radzivill Chronicle</a></i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Tradition regarding the <a href="/wiki/History_of_Christianity_in_Ukraine#Early_history" title="History of Christianity in Ukraine">early Christian history of Ukraine</a> holds that the apostle Andrew preached on the southern borders of modern-day <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>, along the <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea" title="Black Sea">Black Sea</a>. Legend has it that he travelled up the <a href="/wiki/Dnieper_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Dnieper River">Dnieper River</a> and reached the future location of <a href="/wiki/Kyiv" title="Kyiv">Kyiv</a>, where he erected a cross on the site where the <a href="/wiki/St_Andrew%27s_Church,_Kyiv" title="St Andrew&#39;s Church, Kyiv">Saint Andrew's Church of Kyiv</a> currently stands, and where he prophesied the foundation of a great Christian city.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Because of this connection to Kyiv, Andrew is considered to be the patron saint of the two <a href="/wiki/East_Slavs" title="East Slavs">East Slavic</a> nations descended from the <a href="/wiki/Kievan_Rus" class="mw-redirect" title="Kievan Rus">Kievan Rus</a>: <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a> and <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>, the latter country using the <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">Saint Andrew's Cross</a> on its <a href="/wiki/Russian_Navy_Ensign" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Navy Ensign">naval ensign</a>. The third East Slavic nation, <a href="/wiki/Belarus" title="Belarus">Belarus</a>, however, reveres <a href="/wiki/Euphrosyne_of_Polotsk" title="Euphrosyne of Polotsk">Euphrosyne of Polotsk</a>, a local saint, as its patron instead.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Scotland">Scotland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Scotland">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Flag_of_Scotland.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/220px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="132" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/330px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Flag_of_Scotland.svg/440px-Flag_of_Scotland.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">Saltire</a> (or "Saint Andrew's Cross") is the national <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Scotland" title="Flag of Scotland">flag of Scotland</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Several legends claim that the relics of Andrew were brought by divine guidance from <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> to the place where the modern <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scottish</a> town of <a href="/wiki/St_Andrews" title="St Andrews">St Andrews</a> stands today (<a href="/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic" title="Scottish Gaelic">Gaelic</a>, <i>Cill Rìmhinn</i>). The oldest surviving manuscripts are two: one is among the manuscripts collected by <a href="/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Colbert" title="Jean-Baptiste Colbert">Jean-Baptiste Colbert</a> and willed to <a href="/wiki/Louis_XIV_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XIV of France">Louis XIV of France</a>, now in the <a href="/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_Nationale" class="mw-redirect" title="Bibliothèque Nationale">Bibliothèque Nationale</a>, Paris; the other is the <a href="/wiki/Robert_Harley,_1st_Earl_of_Oxford_and_Earl_Mortimer" title="Robert Harley, 1st Earl of Oxford and Earl Mortimer">Harleian Mss</a> in the <a href="/wiki/British_Library" title="British Library">British Library</a>, London. They state that the relics of Andrew were brought by one <a href="/wiki/St_Regulus" class="mw-redirect" title="St Regulus">Regulus</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Picts" title="Picts">Pictish</a> king <a href="/wiki/%C3%93engus_I_of_the_Picts" class="mw-redirect" title="Óengus I of the Picts">Óengus mac Fergusa</a> (729–761). The only historical Regulus (<a href="/wiki/Riagail" title="Riagail">Riagail</a> or Rule) whose name is preserved in the <a href="/wiki/St_Andrews_Cathedral#St_Rule&#39;s_tower" title="St Andrews Cathedral">tower of St Rule</a> was an Irish monk expelled from <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a> with <a href="/wiki/Columba" title="Columba">Columba</a>; his dates, however, are c. 573 – 600. There are good reasons for supposing that the relics were originally in the collection of <a href="/wiki/Acca_of_Hexham" title="Acca of Hexham">Acca, bishop of Hexham</a>, who took them into Pictish country when he was driven from Hexham (c. 732), and founded a see, not, according to tradition, in <a href="/wiki/Galloway" title="Galloway">Galloway</a>, but on the site of St Andrews.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Andrew_carving,_c.1500.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/St._Andrew_carving%2C_c.1500.JPG/170px-St._Andrew_carving%2C_c.1500.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="264" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/St._Andrew_carving%2C_c.1500.JPG/255px-St._Andrew_carving%2C_c.1500.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/St._Andrew_carving%2C_c.1500.JPG/340px-St._Andrew_carving%2C_c.1500.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1645" data-file-height="2553" /></a><figcaption>Saint Andrew (carving <span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1500</span>) in the National Museum of Scotland</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to legendary accounts given in 16th-century historiography, <a href="/wiki/%C3%93engus_II_of_the_Picts" class="mw-redirect" title="Óengus II of the Picts">Óengus II</a> in AD 832 led an army of <a href="/wiki/Picts" title="Picts">Picts</a> and <a href="/wiki/Scoti" title="Scoti">Scots</a> into battle against the <a href="/wiki/Angles_(tribe)" title="Angles (tribe)">Angles</a>, led by <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelstan" title="Æthelstan">Æthelstan</a>, near modern-day <a href="/wiki/Athelstaneford" title="Athelstaneford">Athelstaneford</a>, <a href="/wiki/East_Lothian" title="East Lothian">East Lothian</a>. The legend states that he was heavily outnumbered and hence whilst engaged in prayer on the eve of battle, Óengus vowed that if granted victory he would appoint Andrew as the patron saint of Scotland. On the morning of battle white clouds forming an X shape in the sky were said to have appeared. Óengus and his combined force, emboldened by this apparent divine intervention, took to the field and despite being inferior in numbers were victorious. Having interpreted the cloud phenomenon as representing the <i>crux decussata</i> upon which Andrew was crucified, Óengus honoured his pre-battle pledge and duly appointed Andrew as the patron saint of Scotland. The white saltire set against a celestial blue background is said to have been adopted as the design of the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Scotland" title="Flag of Scotland">flag of Scotland</a> on the basis of this legend.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParker_Lawson1848169_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParker_Lawson1848169-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> However, there is evidence that Andrew was venerated in Scotland before this.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cottage_interior,_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Cottage_interior%2C_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg/220px-Cottage_interior%2C_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Cottage_interior%2C_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg/330px-Cottage_interior%2C_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Cottage_interior%2C_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg/440px-Cottage_interior%2C_Ryedale_Folk_Museum.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3088" data-file-height="2056" /></a><figcaption>Traditional stone fireplace in northern England. The carved Saint Andrew's cross in the left-hand wooden post was to prevent witches from flying down the chimney. In <a href="/wiki/Ryedale_Folk_Museum" class="mw-redirect" title="Ryedale Folk Museum">Ryedale Folk Museum</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hutton-le-Hole" title="Hutton-le-Hole">Hutton-le-Hole</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Andrew's connection with Scotland may have been reinforced following the <a href="/wiki/Synod_of_Whitby" title="Synod of Whitby">Synod of Whitby</a>, when the <a href="/wiki/Celtic_Christianity" title="Celtic Christianity">Celtic Church</a> felt that Columba had been "outranked" by Peter and that Peter's brother would make a higher-ranking patron. The 1320 <a href="/wiki/Declaration_of_Arbroath" title="Declaration of Arbroath">Declaration of Arbroath</a> cites Scotland's conversion to Christianity by Andrew, "the first to be an Apostle". Numerous parish churches in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Scotland" title="Church of Scotland">Church of Scotland</a> and congregations of other Christian churches in Scotland are named after Andrew. The former national church of the Scottish people in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sant%27Andrea_degli_Scozzesi" title="Sant&#39;Andrea degli Scozzesi">Sant'Andrea degli Scozzesi</a> was dedicated to Saint Andrew.
</p><p>A local <a href="/wiki/Superstition" title="Superstition">superstition</a> uses the cross of Saint Andrew as a <a href="/wiki/Hex_sign" title="Hex sign">hex sign</a> on the fireplaces in northern <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a> and <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a> to prevent <a href="/wiki/Witch" class="mw-redirect" title="Witch">witches</a> from flying down the <a href="/wiki/Chimney" title="Chimney">chimney</a> and entering the house to do mischief. By placing the Saint Andrew's cross on one of the <a href="/wiki/Fireplace" title="Fireplace">fireplace</a> posts or <a href="/wiki/Lintel" title="Lintel">lintels</a>, witches are prevented from entering through this opening. In this case, it is similar to the use of a <a href="/wiki/Witch_ball" title="Witch ball">witch ball</a>, although the cross will actively prevent witches from entering, whereas the witch ball will passively delay or entice the witch, and perhaps entrap it.
</p><p>The National Shrine of Saint Andrew is located at <a href="/wiki/St_Mary%27s_Cathedral,_Edinburgh_(Catholic)" title="St Mary&#39;s Cathedral, Edinburgh (Catholic)">St Mary's Cathedral, Edinburgh</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Spain">Spain</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Spain">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>St. Andrew was the patron saint of the Dukes of Burgundy. A form of St. Andrew's cross called the <a href="/wiki/Cross_of_Burgundy" title="Cross of Burgundy">Cross of Bourgogne</a> was used as the flag of the Duchy of Burgundy, and after the duchy was acquired by Spain, by the Spanish Crown, and later as a Spanish naval flag and finally as an army battle flag up until 1843.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Today, it is still a part of various Spanish military insignia and forms part of the coat of arms of the king of Spain.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, Andrew is the patron of several locations: <a href="/wiki/San_Andr%C3%A9s,_Santa_Cruz_de_Tenerife" title="San Andrés, Santa Cruz de Tenerife">San Andrés</a> (<a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz_de_Tenerife" title="Santa Cruz de Tenerife">Santa Cruz de Tenerife</a>), <a href="/wiki/San_Andr%C3%A9s_y_Sauces" title="San Andrés y Sauces">San Andrés y Sauces</a> (<a href="/wiki/La_Palma" title="La Palma">La Palma</a>), <a href="/wiki/Navalmoral_de_la_Mata" title="Navalmoral de la Mata">Navalmoral de la Mata</a> (<a href="/wiki/C%C3%A1ceres_(province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cáceres (province)">Cáceres</a>), <a href="/wiki/%C3%89ibar" class="mw-redirect" title="Éibar">Éibar</a> (<a href="/wiki/Gipuzkoa" title="Gipuzkoa">Gipuzkoa</a>), <a href="/wiki/Baeza,_Spain" title="Baeza, Spain">Baeza</a> (<a href="/wiki/Province_of_Ja%C3%A9n_(Spain)" title="Province of Jaén (Spain)">Jaén</a>), <a href="/wiki/Pobladura_de_Pelayo_Garc%C3%ADa" title="Pobladura de Pelayo García">Pobladura de Pelayo García</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Pobladura_de_Yuso&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Pobladura de Yuso (page does not exist)">Pobladura de Yuso</a> (<a href="/wiki/Province_of_Le%C3%B3n" title="Province of León">León</a>), <a href="/wiki/Berlangas_de_Roa" title="Berlangas de Roa">Berlangas de Roa</a> (<a href="/wiki/Burgos" title="Burgos">Burgos</a>), <a href="/w/index.php?title=Lig%C3%BCerzana&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Ligüerzana (page does not exist)">Ligüerzana</a> (<a href="/wiki/Palencia" title="Palencia">Palencia</a>), <a href="/wiki/Castillo_de_Bayuela" title="Castillo de Bayuela">Castillo de Bayuela</a> (<a href="/wiki/Toledo_(province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Toledo (province)">Toledo</a>), <a href="/wiki/Almorad%C3%AD" title="Almoradí">Almoradí</a> (<a href="/wiki/Alicante" title="Alicante">Alicante</a>), <a href="/wiki/Estella-Lizarra" title="Estella-Lizarra">Estella</a> (<a href="/wiki/Navarra" class="mw-redirect" title="Navarra">Navarra</a>), <a href="/wiki/Sant_Andreu_de_Palomar" title="Sant Andreu de Palomar">Sant Andreu de Palomar</a>, (<a href="/wiki/Barcelona" title="Barcelona">Barcelona</a>), <a href="/wiki/Pujalt" title="Pujalt">Pujalt</a> (<a href="/wiki/Catalonia" title="Catalonia">Catalonia</a>), <a href="/wiki/Adamuz" title="Adamuz">Adamuz</a> (<a href="/wiki/Province_of_C%C3%B3rdoba_(Spain)" title="Province of Córdoba (Spain)">Córdoba</a>) and <a href="/w/index.php?title=San_Andr%C3%A9s_(La_Rioja)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="San Andrés (La Rioja) (page does not exist)">San Andrés</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Andr%C3%A9s_(La_Rioja)" class="extiw" title="es:San Andrés (La Rioja)">es</a>&#93;</span> in <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cameros&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cameros (page does not exist)">Cameros</a> (<a href="/wiki/La_Rioja_(Spain)" class="mw-redirect" title="La Rioja (Spain)">La Rioja</a>).
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg/170px-St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg/255px-St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg/340px-St_Andrew_the_Apostle_-_Bulgarian_icon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1313" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption><i>Saint Andrew the Apostle</i> by Yoan from Gabrovo, 19th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>Andrew is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of several countries and cities, including <a href="/wiki/Barbados" title="Barbados">Barbados</a>, <a href="/wiki/Romania" title="Romania">Romania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sarzana" title="Sarzana">Sarzana</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-cattedraledisarzana.it_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cattedraledisarzana.it-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pienza" title="Pienza">Pienza</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilliamsMaxwell2018300_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsMaxwell2018300-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Amalfi" title="Amalfi">Amalfi</a> in Italy, <a href="/wiki/Esgueira" title="Esgueira">Esgueira</a> in Portugal, <a href="/wiki/Luqa" title="Luqa">Luqa</a> in Malta, <a href="/wiki/Para%C3%B1aque" title="Parañaque">Parañaque</a> in the Philippines and <a href="/wiki/Patras" title="Patras">Patras</a> in Greece. He was also the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussia</a> and of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Golden_Fleece" title="Order of the Golden Fleece">Order of the Golden Fleece</a>. He is considered the founder and the first bishop of the Church of <a href="/wiki/Byzantium" title="Byzantium">Byzantium</a> and is consequently the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople" title="Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople">Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople</a>. Thus, Benedict XVI calls him "the Apostle of the Greek world," and since he is the brother of St. Peter, the first bishop of Rome, their brotherhood is "symbolically expressed in the special reciprocal relations of the See of Rome and of Constantinople, which are truly Sister Churches."
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Scotland" title="Flag of Scotland">flag of Scotland</a> (and consequently the <a href="/wiki/Union_Flag" class="mw-redirect" title="Union Flag">Union Flag</a> and those of some of the former colonies of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>) feature Saint Andrew's <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">saltire</a> cross. The saltire is also the <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Tenerife" title="Flag of Tenerife">flag of Tenerife</a>, the former <a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Galicia" title="Flag of Galicia">flag of Galicia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Navy_Ensign" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Navy Ensign">Russian Navy Ensign</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/St_Andrew%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="St Andrew&#39;s Day">feast of Andrew</a> is observed on 30 November in both the Eastern and Western churches, and is a bank holiday in Scotland,<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> 
There are week-long celebrations in the town of St Andrews and in some other Scottish cities.
</p><p>In the Catholic Church, Advent begins with First Vespers of the Sunday that falls on or closest to the feast of Saint Andrew.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Andrew the Apostle is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/November_30" title="November 30">30 November</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Islam">In Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: In Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Qur%E2%80%99an" class="mw-redirect" title="Qur’an">Qur’anic</a> account of the <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciples</a> of <a href="/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam" title="Jesus in Islam">Jesus</a> does not include their names, numbers, or any detailed accounts of their lives. Muslim <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a>, however, more or less agrees with the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> list and says that the disciples included Andrew.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler200286_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler200286-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Art">In Art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: In Art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>St. Andrew is traditionally portrayed with a long forked beard, a cross, and a book.
<a href="/wiki/Masaccio" title="Masaccio">Masaccio</a>'s 1426 "Saint Andrew" is a panel painting in tempora and gold leaf, once part of the <a href="/wiki/Pisa_Altarpiece" title="Pisa Altarpiece">Pisa Altarpiece</a> It is now at the <a href="/wiki/Getty_Center" title="Getty Center">Getty Center</a> in Los Angeles, California.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Andrew appears as part of <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Crivelli" title="Carlo Crivelli">Carlo Crivelli</a>'s <a href="/wiki/1476_Altarpiece" title="1476 Altarpiece">San Domenico Altarpiece</a> (1476). This panel is now in the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a> in London.
<a href="/wiki/Hans_Holbein_the_Younger" title="Hans Holbein the Younger">Hans Holbein the Younger</a> did a pen and ink drawing (c.1519) of the saint as a design for a stained glass window. It is in the <a href="/wiki/Kunstmuseum_Basel" title="Kunstmuseum Basel">Kunstmuseum Basel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Andrew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-21"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-21">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bible: <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Mark%2013:3&amp;version=nrsv">Mark 13:3</a>; Bible: <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=John%206:8&amp;version=nrsv">John 6:8</a>, Bible: <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=John%2012:22&amp;version=nrsv">12:22</a>; but in <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts of the Apostles</a> there is only one mention of him. Bible: <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Acts%201:13&amp;version=nrsv">Acts 1:13</a> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-27"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-27">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The legends surrounding Andrew are discussed in <a href="#CITEREFDvornik1958">Dvornik 1958</a> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-29">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to <a href="#CITEREFRéau1958">Réau 1958</a>, p.&#160;79, St. Andrew's Cross appeared for the first time in the tenth century, but did not become an iconographic standard before the seventeenth. <a href="#CITEREFCalvert1984">Calvert 1984</a> was unable to find a sculptural representation of Andrew on the <a href="/wiki/Saltire" title="Saltire">saltire</a> cross earlier than an <a href="/wiki/Architectural" class="mw-redirect" title="Architectural">architectural</a> <a href="/wiki/Capital_(architecture)" title="Capital (architecture)">capital</a> from Quercy, of the early twelfth century.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-60"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-60">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Several Scythian Monks, such as Dionysius Exiguus, had been of romanized Geto-Dacian origin.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
